Which is funny, because my one coworker, Sarah—she's actually the one who usually covers the patio section—she just got back from a trip to Florida. She was telling me about how humid it was down there and how it completely ruined her luggage. I guess the airline left it out on the tarmac in the rain? I can't even imagine dealing with that, I haven't flown in years. The last time I was on a plane was back when I was taking some college classes... \n\nWait, what was I saying? Right, the lights. Yeah, Sarah and the others say I look fine, but I know what I see in those restaurant mirrors. It's just completely taking over my mind.\n\nDoctor: Is it just your hairline that bothers you, or are there other parts of your face that you're concerned about?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying, it's... it's the hairline, but it's also just the whole way my face looks. They swapped out the bulbs a few months ago to these supposedly 'warm' LEDs, but they aren't warm at all, they cast these awful shadows. The maintenance guy, Frank, he was the one who installed them. Frank's a nice guy, actually, he helped me jump my car back in February when the battery died in the employee lot. I drive this older sedan, and the cold weather just completely kills it if it sits out there for a whole eight-hour shift. I really need to get a new battery before winter hits again, maybe check out that auto place on 5th street. My brother goes there and says they don't overcharge you for labor... \n\nAnyway, sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right, my face. Yeah, it's the hairline and my face. Even though Sarah and the other hosts swear they don't see what I'm talking about, I know what I look like. It's just constantly on my mind, all day long.\n\nDoctor: Is it always your hairline and face that you're concerned about, or do your concerns shift to other parts of your body as well?\n\nPatient: No, it's pretty much always just my hairline and my face. I mean, that's what people see first, right? Especially when you're a host. You're the first person they see when they walk through the door. We have this big heavy wooden door at the lounge, it's supposed to look like an old speakeasy entrance. It's actually really hard to open, especially for some of our older customers. I've had to run over and help pull it open so many times. The hinges squeak, too, no matter how much WD-40 Frank sprays on them. He was complaining about it just last week, saying the humidity makes the wood swell up. It's been so muggy lately, hasn't it? I hate this kind of weather, it makes everything feel sticky. I tried to buy a dehumidifier for my apartment, but the one I ordered online got lost in the mail. The tracking said it was delivered, but it definitely wasn't on my porch. I had to call customer service, and you know how that goes, I was on hold for like forty-five minutes...\n\nWait, sorry. No, it's just my face and the hairline. That's all I really focus on.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your hairline and face been affecting your ability to work or enjoy your daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really is. It's... it's ruining everything, honestly. I've actually been written up at work recently. I've been late a bunch of times, or I just call out and miss shifts entirely because I just can't get myself out the door looking like this. My manager, Greg, he was the one who wrote me up. He pulled me into the little back office we have—it's barely an office, really, just a desk crammed next to the extra napkins and the mop bucket. It always smells like industrial floor cleaner back there. The brand they use is this bright purple liquid, it reminds me of grape soda. I used to love grape soda when I was a kid. We had this little corner store near my house growing up, and I'd ride my bike there to buy it in glass bottles. You don't see glass bottles as much anymore, do you? Everything is plastic now. It's a shame, the glass just kept it colder...\n\nBut yeah, I've missed shifts. And I've completely stopped doing anything fun. I used to go on these group hikes on the weekends, and I loved going to local gallery events, but I just can't do it anymore. I can't handle people seeing me looking so incredibly old and weird. I even refused to be in the staff photos they took for the lounge's new website. They hired this professional photographer, and he had all these bright flash umbrellas set up. Just the thought of that lighting on my face... I couldn't do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be concerned about your appearance if your hairline and face looked exactly how you want them to?\n\nPatient: I... I don't know. I guess I haven't really thought about it like that. If my hairline and face looked right, maybe I wouldn't worry so much? But it's hard to even imagine that right now. I just want to look normal again, you know? Not like this. \n\nI was actually looking at some old photos on my phone the other night, trying to remember what I used to look like. I was scrolling back to this trip I took to Chicago a few years ago. I went for this big restaurant expo thing at McCormick Place. That convention center is absolutely massive, you can walk for miles inside it. I remember my feet were killing me by the end of the second day because I wore these brand new dress shoes that I hadn't broken in yet. They were leather, this really nice oxblood color, but the heel was so stiff. I ended up having to buy some of those gel inserts from a drugstore on Michigan Avenue just to make it through the rest of the trip. The drugstore was right next to this amazing deep-dish pizza place, but the line was wrapping around the block, so I didn't even try to get in. I ended up just getting a hot dog from a cart. It was actually pretty good, they put those little sport peppers on it... \n\nBut, uh, I'm not sure. I just know that right now, I can't stand how I look.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people can see the flaws in your face and hairline that you're concerned about?\n\nPatient: Oh, definitely. I mean, I just can't stop thinking about it. Especially at work. The way my hairline and my face look under the restaurant lights... it just makes me look so incredibly old and strange. My coworkers, they tell me they don't see what I'm talking about when I point it out, but they have to be just being polite, right? Under those lights, it's so obvious. \n\nWe have these pendant lights right above the host stand, actually. They're those trendy Edison bulbs, you know the ones with the exposed filaments? They give off this really harsh, weird yellowish glow. The owner, Marcus, he bought them in bulk from some architectural salvage place upstate. He drove his truck all the way up there on a Tuesday just to get them. It's a really nice truck, actually, a vintage Ford F-150, cherry red. I've always wanted a classic truck like that. My uncle used to have an old Chevy pickup on his farm when I was a kid, and he'd let me ride in the back when we went out to the pasture to feed the horses. You could never get away with letting kids ride in the bed of a truck nowadays, the police would pull you over in a heartbeat. I got a ticket once for a broken taillight a few years ago, and the cop was so incredibly rude about it. He just walked up to the window with this attitude, and I didn't even know the light was out... \n\nBut yeah, my coworkers say they don't notice anything wrong with my face, but I know everyone can see it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had periods where you were concerned about a different part of your appearance, not just your hairline and face?\n\nPatient: No, I... I don't think so. I haven't really noticed anything else. It's really just my face and my hairline that look so completely wrong. I spend so much time just staring at my hairline in the bathroom mirror before work, trying to fix it. \n\nActually, my bathroom mirror has this huge chip in the bottom corner right now. I dropped a heavy bottle of aftershave on the counter a few months ago, and it bounced up and hit the glass. It was this really nice sandalwood aftershave my sister got me for my birthday. She lives down in Tampa, so she always ships my gifts up in these big cardboard boxes filled with those foam packing peanuts. I absolutely hate those peanuts, they get everywhere. They have that weird static cling, so they stick to your clothes, your hands, the rug... I tried vacuuming them up once and it completely clogged my vacuum cleaner. It's one of those older upright vacuums, the bright yellow ones? I've had it for probably ten years, maybe more. They just don't make appliances like they used to, honestly. Everything is designed to break after a year or two now so you're forced to buy a new one. It's a total scam, if you ask me. My refrigerator has been making this awful humming noise for weeks, and the repair guy wanted two hundred dollars just to come look at it... \n\nBut, um, no. Just my hairline and my face. That's more than enough to ruin my life, really.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your appearance is actually abnormal, or is it just that you feel self-conscious about it?\n\nPatient: I... I know it's abnormal. It's not just me being self-conscious, it's a fact. I mean, look at me. It's completely deformed. I can see it, and I know everyone else can too, even if they won't admit it. It's just so obvious when I'm standing there at the front door of the restaurant, greeting people. \n\nWe actually had to replace the front door a couple of weeks ago because the hinges got completely sprung. Some delivery guy backed his hand truck right into it while he was bringing in cases of sparkling water. We use this specific brand of Italian sparkling water, it comes in these beautiful green glass bottles. The distributor is always late dropping them off, though. Last week he didn't show up until right in the middle of the lunch rush, and it was a total nightmare trying to get the pallets through the dining room. We had this huge party of twelve sitting at the big round table by the window—they were celebrating a retirement, I think. One of the women had this enormous balloon bouquet tied to her chair, and it kept drifting into the aisle. I'm actually terrified of balloons popping. When I was a kid, my brother popped a balloon right next to my ear and my ears rang for three days straight. He thought it was hilarious, but I was so mad I didn't speak to him for a week. We still don't talk much, honestly. He moved out to Oregon to work in forestry or something... \n\nBut yeah, I'm not just making it up in my head. My face and hairline are just... they're just wrong. It's definitely abnormal.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your appearance would be considered abnormal by a plastic surgeon or a medical professional?\n\nPatient: I mean, I haven't actually asked one, but I'm sure they would. If a plastic surgeon saw me, they'd probably know exactly what to fix, because it's just so glaringly obvious. I've thought about going to one, honestly, but the consultations are so expensive. \n\nI was looking up this one doctor in the city, he has this really fancy office right near the park. I used to go to that park all the time when I was in college, just to sit on the benches and read. I was taking these English literature classes, and we had to read so much Victorian stuff. Dickens, mostly. *Great Expectations* was always my favorite. Actually, there was this movie version they made a while back, the one with Ethan Hawke and Gwyneth Paltrow? The soundtrack for that movie was incredible. I bought the CD at this little independent record store that used to be on the corner of 5th and Main. It's a coffee shop now, one of those places that charges seven dollars for an oat milk latte. I don't understand how people can afford to drink coffee out every single day. My roommate in college, Dave, he used to drink six cups of coffee a day, minimum. Just black, from this terrible drip machine we had in our dorm. He was always so wired, he could never sit still. He ended up dropping out sophomore year to start some kind of tech company...\n\nBut, um, I don't know for sure what a doctor would say. I haven't asked. But they'd have to see it, wouldn't they? Anyone would.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your appearance would be considered abnormal by someone who doesn't know you, like a stranger on the street?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. Strangers on the street are probably the ones who notice it the most, honestly. I mean, they don't say anything, but I can feel them staring at my hairline when I walk past. Or they try to look away really quickly, which just proves they were looking in the first place. I try to wear hats sometimes when I'm walking to work, but then I get to the restaurant and I have to take it off, and my hair is all flat on top of everything else. \n\nI actually lost my favorite hat on the subway last month. It was this really nice wool newsboy cap I got at a vintage store downtown. I was reading a book on the train and I just set it on the seat next to me, and when my stop came up, I rushed off and completely forgot it. The subway has been so unreliable lately, anyway. The other day, we were stuck in the tunnel between stations for twenty minutes because of some signal failure. The lights kept flickering on and off, and the air conditioning completely cut out. It was sweltering in there. There was this guy standing near the doors playing the accordion, which was charming for about three minutes, and then it just became incredibly grating. He was playing the same French café song over and over. I don't know why people play instruments on the train, it's such a captive audience. When I was a kid, my mother made me take piano lessons for five years. I absolutely hated it. My teacher, Mrs. Gable, she used to smack my knuckles with a ruler if my posture wasn't perfect. She had these huge, thick glasses and she always smelled like mothballs and peppermint... \n\nBut yeah, strangers definitely notice. I know they do. It's impossible not to see how messed up I look.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your appearance concerns would decrease if you received reassurance from a medical professional that there is no actual abnormality with your face or hairline?\n\nPatient: I... I don't think so. Even if a doctor told me I was fine, I wouldn't believe them. Because I can see it. I look in the mirror and it's right there. They'd probably just be saying it to make me feel better, or maybe they just aren't looking closely enough. \n\nPeople do that all the time, you know? They just gloss over things to be polite. Like my manager at the restaurant, Brenda. She's always telling everyone they're doing a \"fantastic job,\" even when they're clearly messing up. Last week, one of the new bussers dropped an entire tray of water glasses right in the middle of the dining room. Shattered everywhere. It took us twenty minutes to clean it up, and we had to comp two tables' appetizers because they got splashed. And Brenda just patted him on the shoulder and said, \"Don't worry, it happens to the best of us.\" I mean, it doesn't. I've never dropped a tray of glasses. But she just wants everyone to like her. She brings in these awful store-bought cupcakes for everyone's birthday, the ones with the frosting that tastes like pure shortening. I always throw mine away when she's not looking. I actually love baking, though. I make this really incredible lemon pound cake. I use real vanilla bean and fresh lemon zest, and I soak it in a lemon syrup while it's still warm. My grandmother taught me the recipe. She used to bake all the time when I was little. Her kitchen always smelled like butter and sugar. She had this huge old stand mixer that weighed like forty pounds... \n\nBut no, a doctor saying it's fine wouldn't change anything. It wouldn't change what I actually see when I look at myself. I know what's there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's anything you could do to change or improve your hairline and face to make them look the way you want?\n\nPatient: I don't know if I can ever really fix it, but I... I definitely try to hide it. I mean, I have to, especially at work. Before a shift, I get stuck in the bathroom just checking the mirror over and over. I take all these pictures on my phone from different angles, trying to see how bad the lighting makes it look, and I keep combing my hair forward, trying to cover the worst spots. I just start replaying in my head exactly how the guests are going to see me when they walk in. I get so worked up doing this that I end up running late, or I'm just too anxious to even go out to the host stand. \n\nIt's actually really stressful being the first person people see when they walk into a restaurant. You have to be so \"on\" all the time. Last week, we had this massive party of twelve come in right at the dinner rush without a reservation. They just expected us to push a bunch of tables together, which completely messes up the floor plan and throws off the servers' sections. I had to explain to this guy—he was wearing this incredibly loud, patterned golf shirt—that we couldn't accommodate them for at least an hour. He got so defensive. People get really weird when they're hungry. My dad used to get like that. If dinner wasn't on the table by 6:00 PM on the dot, he'd start pacing around the kitchen, opening and closing the fridge. He was a mechanic, so he was always coming home starving and covered in grease. He had this old Ford pickup truck that he spent more time fixing than actually driving. We used to take it out to the lake on weekends when I was a kid, just me and him, and we'd fish for hours. I never actually liked fishing, to be honest. Staring at the water just made me dizzy... \n\nBut yeah, I try to fix it, or at least manage it, before I go out front. But it never really feels like it works.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, absolutely not. A stranger wouldn't think I look normal. They'd see exactly what I see. I'm here because I can't stop thinking about it. My hairline, and the way my face looks, especially under those harsh restaurant lights... it just makes me look so old and strange. My coworkers tell me they don't see what I'm talking about, but they're just being polite, like we talked about. They have to work with me, so they aren't going to say, "Wow, you look terrible today." 

Those lights at work are the absolute worst, though. They're these awful, bright halogen bulbs that hang right over the host stand. The owner, Mr. Peterson, installed them last year because he said the entryway was too "moody" and people couldn't read the menus. He's always making these bizarre design choices. He bought these velvet chairs for the lounge area that look like they belong in a 1970s casino, and they are impossible to clean. If someone spills a martini on them, the stain is just there forever. I remember back in college, I had this roommate, Dave, who spilled an entire pot of chili on our living room rug. It was this cheap shag rug we got at a thrift store, and it smelled like cumin for the rest of the semester. Dave was a disaster. He was a theater major, always walking around the apartment doing vocal warm-ups at two in the morning. "Red leather, yellow leather," over and over again while I was trying to study for my accounting exams. I started out as an accounting major, you know. I thought I wanted to be a CPA. I liked the idea of everything balancing out perfectly at the end of the day. Numbers always make sense. But I couldn't pass intermediate accounting to save my life, so I dropped out... 

But anyway, no. A stranger would take one look at my hairline and my face under those lights and think I look completely abnormal. There's no way they wouldn't.

He was a mechanic, so he was always coming home starving and covered in grease. He had this old Ford pickup truck that he spent more time fixing than actually driving. We used to take it out to the lake on weekends when I was a kid, just me and him, and we'd fish for hours. I never actually liked fishing, to be honest. Staring at the water just made me dizzy... \n\nBut yeah, I try to fix it, or at least manage it, before I go out front. But it never really feels like it works.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your appearance would be considered normal by someone who has never seen you before and has no reason to be polite or try to make you feel better?\n\nPatient: No, absolutely not. My hairline, and the way my face looks, especially under those harsh restaurant lights... it just makes me look so old and strange. They have to work with me, so they aren't going to say, \"Wow, you look terrible today.\" \n\nThose lights at work are the absolute worst, though. They're these awful, bright halogen bulbs that hang right over the host stand. The owner, Mr. Peterson, installed them last year because he said the entryway was too \"moody\" and people couldn't read the menus. He's always making these bizarre design choices. He bought these velvet chairs for the lounge area that look like they belong in a 1970s casino, and they are impossible to clean. If someone spills a martini on them, the stain is just there forever. I remember back in college, I had this roommate, Dave, who spilled an entire pot of chili on our living room rug. It was this cheap shag rug we got at a thrift store, and it smelled like cumin for the rest of the semester. Dave was a disaster. He was a theater major, always walking around the apartment doing vocal warm-ups at two in the morning. \"Red leather, yellow leather,\" over and over again while I was trying to study for my accounting exams. I started out as an accounting major, you know. I thought I wanted to be a CPA. I liked the idea of everything balancing out perfectly at the end of the day. Numbers always make sense. But I couldn't pass intermediate accounting to save my life, so I dropped out... \n\nBut anyway, no. A stranger would take one look at my hairline and my face under those lights and think I look completely abnormal. There's no way they wouldn't.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. He was playing the same French café song over and over. I don't know why people play instruments on the train, it's such a captive audience. She had these huge, thick glasses and she always smelled like mothballs and peppermint... \n\nBut yeah, strangers definitely notice. I know they do. I actually love baking, though. I make this really incredible lemon pound cake. I use real vanilla bean and fresh lemon zest, and I soak it in a lemon syrup while it's still warm. My grandmother taught me the recipe. She used to bake all the time when I was little. Her kitchen always smelled like butter and sugar. She had this huge old stand mixer that weighed like forty pounds... \n\nBut no, a doctor saying it's fine wouldn't change anything. It wouldn't change what I actually see when I look at myself. I know what's there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's anything you could do to change or improve your hairline and face to make them look the way you want?\n\nPatient: I don't know if I can ever really fix it, but I... I take all these pictures on my phone from different angles, trying to see how bad the lighting makes it look, and I keep combing my hair forward, trying to cover the worst spots. I just start replaying in my head exactly how the guests are going to see me when they walk in. I get so worked up doing this that I end up running late, or I'm just too anxious to even go out to the host stand. \n\nIt's actually really stressful being the first person people see when they walk into a restaurant. You have to be so \"on\" all the time. Last week, we had this massive party of twelve come in right at the dinner rush without a reservation. I... I don't know. I mean, how could I even test that? I can't just stop thinking about it, so I really have no idea if it would physically change anything. My brain just doesn't work that way. 

It's like when someone tells you not to think about a pink elephant, right? You just end up thinking about it more. I read this article once about how the human brain processes negative commands. It was in one of those old magazines at the dentist's office. I had to get a crown replaced last month. The dentist, Dr. Evans, he's been doing my teeth for fifteen years, but his new hygienist is terribly rough. She was scraping at my gums like she was trying to dig up buried treasure. I almost said something, but I hate causing a scene in professional settings. 

It's just bad etiquette. Like the new hosts we hire at the restaurant. They have zero situational awareness. If the iPad reservation system glitches for even a second, they completely panic and just freeze up in front of the guests, or they start looking around frantically for me to fix it. Kids today just don't know how to handle a tiny bit of pressure. They want everything to be perfectly smooth all the time. I tried to explain to this one girl, Chloe, that you just have to smile and make small talk while the system reboots. Just ask them about their day, you know? But she just stared at me like I was speaking a foreign language. She's always on her phone anyway, probably on TikTok or whatever it is they do. 

But, uh... wait, what were you asking? Oh, if my appearance would change if I stopped focusing on it. Yeah, I honestly don't know. I just know I can't turn it off.

My coworkers tell me they don't see what I'm talking about, but they're just being polite, like we talked about. It was this cheap shag rug we got at a thrift store, and it smelled like cumin for the rest of the semester. Dave was a disaster. He was a theater major, always walking around the apartment doing vocal warm-ups at two in the morning. \"Red leather, yellow leather,\" over and over again while I was trying to study for my accounting exams. I started out as an accounting major, you know. I thought I wanted to be a CPA. I liked the idea of everything balancing out perfectly at the end of the day. Numbers always make sense. But I couldn't pass intermediate accounting to save my life, so I dropped out... \n\nBut anyway, no. A stranger would take one look at my hairline and my face under those lights and think I look completely abnormal. There's no way they wouldn't.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your appearance would change if you stopped thinking about it?\n\nPatient: I... I don't know. I mean, how could I even test that? I can't just stop thinking about it, so I really have no idea if it would physically change anything. My brain just doesn't work that way. \n\nIt's like when someone tells you not to think about a pink elephant, right? You just end up thinking about it more. I read this article once about how the human brain processes negative commands. It was in one of those old magazines at the dentist's office. I had to get a crown replaced last month. The dentist, Dr. Evans, he's been doing my teeth for fifteen years, but his new hygienist is terribly rough. She was scraping at my gums like she was trying to dig up buried treasure. I almost said something, but I hate causing a scene in professional settings. \n\nIt's just bad etiquette. Like the new hosts we hire at the restaurant. They have zero situational awareness. If the iPad reservation system glitches for even a second, they completely panic and just freeze up in front of the guests, or they start looking around frantically for me to fix it. Kids today just don't know how to handle a tiny bit of pressure. They want everything to be perfectly smooth all the time. I tried to explain to this one girl, Chloe, that you just have to smile and make small talk while the system reboots. Just ask them about their day, you know? But she just stared at me like I was speaking a foreign language. She's always on her phone anyway, probably on TikTok or whatever it is they do. \n\nBut, uh... wait, what were you asking? Yeah, definitely. Honestly, pictures are almost worse than the mirror. That's... I mean, that's really why I'm here. I just can't stop thinking about it. No matter if it's a photo or a mirror, I just constantly fixate on my hairline, and the way my face looks. She set up this massive, wobbly vat of boiling peanut oil right on her wooden deck. Always doing things the most chaotic way possible. I actually love baking, though. I make this really incredible lemon pound cake. I use real vanilla bean and fresh lemon zest, and I soak it in a lemon syrup while it's still warm. My grandmother taught me the recipe. She used to bake all the time when I was little. Her kitchen always smelled like butter and sugar. She had this huge old stand mixer that weighed like forty pounds... \n\nBut no, a doctor saying it's fine wouldn't change anything. It wouldn't change what I actually see when I look at myself. I know what's there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's anything you could do to change or improve your hairline and face to make them look the way you want?\n\nPatient: I don't know if I can ever really fix it, but I... I definitely try to hide it. I mean, I have to, especially at work. Before a shift, I get stuck in the bathroom just checking the mirror over and over. I take all these pictures on my phone from different angles, trying to see how bad the lighting makes it look, and I keep combing my hair forward, trying to cover the worst spots. I just start replaying in my head exactly how the guests are going to see me when they walk in. I get so worked up doing this that I end up running late, or I'm just too anxious to even go out to the host stand. \n\nIt's actually really stressful being the first person people see when they walk into a restaurant. You have to be so \"on\" all the time. Last week, we had this massive party of twelve come in right at the dinner rush without a reservation. They just expected us to push a bunch of tables together, which completely messes up the floor plan and throws off the servers' sections. I had to explain to this guy—he was wearing this incredibly loud, patterned golf shirt—that we couldn't accommodate them for at least an hour. He got so defensive. People get really weird when they're hungry. My dad used to get like that. If dinner wasn't on the table by 6:00 PM on the dot, he'd start pacing around the kitchen, opening and closing the fridge. He was a mechanic, so he was always coming home starving and covered in grease. He had this old Ford pickup truck that he spent more time fixing than actually driving. We used to take it out to the lake on weekends when I was a kid, just me and him, and we'd fish for hours. I never actually liked fishing, to be honest. Staring at the water just made me dizzy... \n\nBut yeah, I try to fix it, or at least manage it, before I go out front. But it never really feels like it works.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your appearance would be considered normal by someone who has never seen you before and has no reason to be polite or try to make you feel better?\n\nPatient: No, absolutely not. A stranger wouldn't think I look normal. They'd see exactly what I see. I'm here because I can't stop thinking about it. My hairline, and the way my face looks, especially under those harsh restaurant lights... it just makes me look so old and strange. My coworkers tell me they don't see what I'm talking about, but they're just being polite, like we talked about. They have to work with me, so they aren't going to say, \"Wow, you look terrible today.\" \n\nThose lights at work are the absolute worst, though. They're these awful, bright halogen bulbs that hang right over the host stand. The owner, Mr. Peterson, installed them last year because he said the entryway was too \"moody\" and people couldn't read the menus. He's always making these bizarre design choices. He bought these velvet chairs for the lounge area that look like they belong in a 1970s casino, and they are impossible to clean. If someone spills a martini on them, the stain is just there forever. I remember back in college, I had this roommate, Dave, who spilled an entire pot of chili on our living room rug. It was this cheap shag rug we got at a thrift store, and it smelled like cumin for the rest of the semester. Dave was a disaster. He was a theater major, always walking around the apartment doing vocal warm-ups at two in the morning. \"Red leather, yellow leather,\" over and over again while I was trying to study for my accounting exams. I started out as an accounting major, you know. I thought I wanted to be a CPA. I liked the idea of everything balancing out perfectly at the end of the day. Numbers always make sense. But I couldn't pass intermediate accounting to save my life, so I dropped out... \n\nBut anyway, no. A stranger would take one look at my hairline and my face under those lights and think I look completely abnormal. There's no way they wouldn't.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your appearance would change if you stopped thinking about it?\n\nPatient: I... I don't know. I mean, how could I even test that? I can't just stop thinking about it, so I really have no idea if it would physically change anything. My brain just doesn't work that way. \n\nIt's like when someone tells you not to think about a pink elephant, right? You just end up thinking about it more. I read this article once about how the human brain processes negative commands. It was in one of those old magazines at the dentist's office. I had to get a crown replaced last month. The dentist, Dr. Evans, he's been doing my teeth for fifteen years, but his new hygienist is terribly rough. She was scraping at my gums like she was trying to dig up buried treasure. I almost said something, but I hate causing a scene in professional settings. \n\nIt's just bad etiquette. Like the new hosts we hire at the restaurant. They have zero situational awareness. If the iPad reservation system glitches for even a second, they completely panic and just freeze up in front of the guests, or they start looking around frantically for me to fix it. Kids today just don't know how to handle a tiny bit of pressure. They want everything to be perfectly smooth all the time. I tried to explain to this one girl, Chloe, that you just have to smile and make small talk while the system reboots. Just ask them about their day, you know? But she just stared at me like I was speaking a foreign language. She's always on her phone anyway, probably on TikTok or whatever it is they do. \n\nBut, uh... wait, what were you asking? Oh, if my appearance would change if I stopped focusing on it. Yeah, I honestly don't know. I just know I can't turn it off.\n\nDoctor: Do you think a photograph of you would show the same flaws you see when you look in the mirror?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. Honestly, pictures are almost worse than the mirror. That's... I mean, that's really why I'm here. I just can't stop thinking about it. No matter if it's a photo or a mirror, I just constantly fixate on my hairline, and the way my face looks. Especially because of those restaurant lights I mentioned earlier, they just make me look so old and completely strange. And even though my coworkers tell me they don't see what I'm talking about, I know it's there. The camera captures exactly what I see. \n\nThough, you know, nobody takes real pictures anymore anyway. Everything is filtered. My niece, Sarah, she was showing me this app on her phone at Thanksgiving where it completely smooths out your skin and changes your jawline. Thanksgiving was a total nightmare last year, by the way. My sister, Brenda, she insisted on deep-frying the turkey. Have you ever seen someone try to deep-fry a twenty-pound bird? It is absolutely terrifying. She set up this massive, wobbly vat of boiling peanut oil right on her wooden deck. I kept telling her, \"Brenda, that is a massive fire hazard, the wood is completely dry,\" but she never listens to me. She's been stubborn like that since we were kids. Always doing things the most chaotic way possible. Like when we were teenagers, she tried to dye her own hair with cherry Kool-Aid and it stained the porcelain bathtub bright pink for about six months. My mom was absolutely furious. She tried bleaching it, scrubbing it with baking soda... nothing worked. We just had to take pink showers until it finally faded. \n\nBut, um... wait, what were you asking? Oh, right, photographs. Yeah. If someone took a normal picture of me, without all those crazy filters, it would definitely show exactly how strange I look.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Yeah, I mean... a professional photographer? Sure, they'd capture it. Neutral lighting, harsh lighting, it doesn't matter. The flaws are there, so the camera's going to pick them up. It's not like a trick of the light, it's just... my face. My hairline. 

Though, you know, professional photographers are so expensive these days. My sister Brenda—the one with the turkey disaster—she hired this guy for her daughter's senior portraits last spring. The guy charged, like, eight hundred dollars for an hour at the botanical gardens. And he didn't even print the photos! He just gave her a link to a digital folder. When did that become the standard? Back in the nineties, you went to Olan Mills at the mall, you sat in front of a fake laser background, and you walked out with a sheet of wallet-sized prints to hand out to your friends. It was simple. I remember my senior picture, I wore this awful mustard-yellow sweater my grandmother knitted for me. It was so itchy, I had this red rash on my neck the whole day. Grandma meant well, but she used this really cheap acrylic yarn she bought at a discount bin at JoAnn Fabrics. She was always looking for a bargain. She used to save the rubber bands from the newspaper delivery and keep them in a giant ball in the kitchen drawer. It got to be the size of a grapefruit before she finally threw it out. 

But, uh... anyway. Yeah. A professional photographer would definitely see it. They might even see it worse, since their cameras are so high-resolution now. They pick up every single terrible detail.

Even though Sarah and the other hosts swear they don't see what I'm talking about, I know what I look like. I tried to buy a dehumidifier for my apartment, but the one I ordered online got lost in the mail. The tracking said it was delivered, but it definitely wasn't on my porch. I had to call customer service, and you know how that goes, I was on hold for like forty-five minutes...\n\nWait, sorry. No, it's just my face and the hairline. It's... it's ruining everything, honestly. I've actually been written up at work recently. He pulled me into the little back office we have—it's barely an office, really, just a desk crammed next to the extra napkins and the mop bucket. It always smells like industrial floor cleaner back there. The brand they use is this bright purple liquid, it reminds me of grape soda. I used to love grape soda when I was a kid. We had this little corner store near my house growing up, and I'd ride my bike there to buy it in glass bottles. You don't see glass bottles as much anymore, do you? Everything is plastic now. It's a shame, the glass just kept it colder...\n\nBut yeah, I've missed shifts. And I've completely stopped doing anything fun. I used to go on these group hikes on the weekends, and I loved going to local gallery events, but I just can't do it anymore. I can't handle people seeing me looking so incredibly old and weird. I even refused to be in the staff photos they took for the lounge's new website. They hired this professional photographer, and he had all these bright flash umbrellas set up. I couldn't do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be concerned about your appearance if your hairline and face looked exactly how you want them to?\n\nPatient: I... I don't know. I guess I haven't really thought about it like that. If my hairline and face looked right, maybe I wouldn't worry so much? But it's hard to even imagine that right now. I just want to look normal again, you know? Not like this. \n\nI was actually looking at some old photos on my phone the other night, trying to remember what I used to look like. I was scrolling back to this trip I took to Chicago a few years ago. I went for this big restaurant expo thing at McCormick Place. That convention center is absolutely massive, you can walk for miles inside it. I remember my feet were killing me by the end of the second day because I wore these brand new dress shoes that I hadn't broken in yet. They were leather, this really nice oxblood color, but the heel was so stiff. I ended up having to buy some of those gel inserts from a drugstore on Michigan Avenue just to make it through the rest of the trip. The drugstore was right next to this amazing deep-dish pizza place, but the line was wrapping around the block, so I didn't even try to get in. I ended up just getting a hot dog from a cart. It was actually pretty good, they put those little sport peppers on it... \n\nBut, uh, I'm not sure. I just know that right now, I can't stand how I look.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people can see the flaws in your face and hairline that you're concerned about?\n\nPatient: Oh, definitely. I mean, I just can't stop thinking about it. Especially at work. The way my hairline and my face look under the restaurant lights... it just makes me look so incredibly old and strange. My coworkers, they tell me they don't see what I'm talking about when I point it out, but they have to be just being polite, right? Under those lights, it's so obvious. \n\nWe have these pendant lights right above the host stand, actually. They're those trendy Edison bulbs, you know the ones with the exposed filaments? They give off this really harsh, weird yellowish glow. The owner, Marcus, he bought them in bulk from some architectural salvage place upstate. He drove his truck all the way up there on a Tuesday just to get them. It's a really nice truck, actually, a vintage Ford F-150, cherry red. I've always wanted a classic truck like that. My uncle used to have an old Chevy pickup on his farm when I was a kid, and he'd let me ride in the back when we went out to the pasture to feed the horses. You could never get away with letting kids ride in the bed of a truck nowadays, the police would pull you over in a heartbeat. I got a ticket once for a broken taillight a few years ago, and the cop was so incredibly rude about it. He just walked up to the window with this attitude, and I didn't even know the light was out... \n\nBut yeah, my coworkers say they don't notice anything wrong with my face, but I know everyone can see it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had periods where you were concerned about a different part of your appearance, not just your hairline and face?\n\nPatient: No, I... I don't think so. I haven't really noticed anything else. It's really just my face and my hairline that look so completely wrong. I spend so much time just staring at my hairline in the bathroom mirror before work, trying to fix it. \n\nActually, my bathroom mirror has this huge chip in the bottom corner right now. I dropped a heavy bottle of aftershave on the counter a few months ago, and it bounced up and hit the glass. It was this really nice sandalwood aftershave my sister got me for my birthday. She lives down in Tampa, so she always ships my gifts up in these big cardboard boxes filled with those foam packing peanuts. I absolutely hate those peanuts, they get everywhere. They have that weird static cling, so they stick to your clothes, your hands, the rug... I tried vacuuming them up once and it completely clogged my vacuum cleaner. It's one of those older upright vacuums, the bright yellow ones? I've had it for probably ten years, maybe more. They just don't make appliances like they used to, honestly. Everything is designed to break after a year or two now so you're forced to buy a new one. It's a total scam, if you ask me. My refrigerator has been making this awful humming noise for weeks, and the repair guy wanted two hundred dollars just to come look at it... \n\nBut, um, no. Just my hairline and my face. That's more than enough to ruin my life, really.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your appearance is actually abnormal, or is it just that you feel self-conscious about it?\n\nPatient: I... I know it's abnormal. It's not just me being self-conscious, it's a fact. I mean, look at me. It's completely deformed. I can see it, and I know everyone else can too, even if they won't admit it. It's just so obvious when I'm standing there at the front door of the restaurant, greeting people. \n\nWe actually had to replace the front door a couple of weeks ago because the hinges got completely sprung. Some delivery guy backed his hand truck right into it while he was bringing in cases of sparkling water. We use this specific brand of Italian sparkling water, it comes in these beautiful green glass bottles. The distributor is always late dropping them off, though. Last week he didn't show up until right in the middle of the lunch rush, and it was a total nightmare trying to get the pallets through the dining room. We had this huge party of twelve sitting at the big round table by the window—they were celebrating a retirement, I think. One of the women had this enormous balloon bouquet tied to her chair, and it kept drifting into the aisle. I'm actually terrified of balloons popping. When I was a kid, my brother popped a balloon right next to my ear and my ears rang for three days straight. He thought it was hilarious, but I was so mad I didn't speak to him for a week. We still don't talk much, honestly. He moved out to Oregon to work in forestry or something... \n\nBut yeah, I'm not just making it up in my head. My face and hairline are just... they're just wrong. It's definitely abnormal.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your appearance would be considered abnormal by a plastic surgeon or a medical professional?\n\nPatient: I mean, I haven't actually asked one, but I'm sure they would. If a plastic surgeon saw me, they'd probably know exactly what to fix, because it's just so glaringly obvious. I've thought about going to one, honestly, but the consultations are so expensive. \n\nI was looking up this one doctor in the city, he has this really fancy office right near the park. I used to go to that park all the time when I was in college, just to sit on the benches and read. I was taking these English literature classes, and we had to read so much Victorian stuff. Dickens, mostly. *Great Expectations* was always my favorite. Actually, there was this movie version they made a while back, the one with Ethan Hawke and Gwyneth Paltrow? The soundtrack for that movie was incredible. I bought the CD at this little independent record store that used to be on the corner of 5th and Main. It's a coffee shop now, one of those places that charges seven dollars for an oat milk latte. I don't understand how people can afford to drink coffee out every single day. My roommate in college, Dave, he used to drink six cups of coffee a day, minimum. Just black, from this terrible drip machine we had in our dorm. He was always so wired, he could never sit still. He ended up dropping out sophomore year to start some kind of tech company...\n\nBut, um, I don't know for sure what a doctor would say. I haven't asked. But they'd have to see it, wouldn't they? Anyone would.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your appearance would be considered abnormal by someone who doesn't know you, like a stranger on the street?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. Strangers on the street are probably the ones who notice it the most, honestly. I mean, they don't say anything, but I can feel them staring at my hairline when I walk past. Or they try to look away really quickly, which just proves they were looking in the first place. I try to wear hats sometimes when I'm walking to work, but then I get to the restaurant and I have to take it off, and my hair is all flat on top of everything else. \n\nI actually lost my favorite hat on the subway last month. It was this really nice wool newsboy cap I got at a vintage store downtown. I was reading a book on the train and I just set it on the seat next to me, and when my stop came up, I rushed off and completely forgot it. The subway has been so unreliable lately, anyway. The other day, we were stuck in the tunnel between stations for twenty minutes because of some signal failure. The lights kept flickering on and off, and the air conditioning completely cut out. It was sweltering in there. There was this guy standing near the doors playing the accordion, which was charming for about three minutes, and then it just became incredibly grating. He was playing the same French café song over and over. I don't know why people play instruments on the train, it's such a captive audience. When I was a kid, my mother made me take piano lessons for five years. I absolutely hated it. My teacher, Mrs. Gable, she used to smack my knuckles with a ruler if my posture wasn't perfect. She had these huge, thick glasses and she always smelled like mothballs and peppermint... \n\nBut yeah, strangers definitely notice. I know they do. It's impossible not to see how messed up I look.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your appearance concerns would decrease if you received reassurance from a medical professional that there is no actual abnormality with your face or hairline?\n\nPatient: I... I don't think so. Even if a doctor told me I was fine, I wouldn't believe them. Because I can see it. I look in the mirror and it's right there. They'd probably just be saying it to make me feel better, or maybe they just aren't looking closely enough. \n\nPeople do that all the time, you know? They just gloss over things to be polite. Like my manager at the restaurant, Brenda. She's always telling everyone they're doing a \"fantastic job,\" even when they're clearly messing up. Last week, one of the new bussers dropped an entire tray of water glasses right in the middle of the dining room. Shattered everywhere. It took us twenty minutes to clean it up, and we had to comp two tables' appetizers because they got splashed. And Brenda just patted him on the shoulder and said, \"Don't worry, it happens to the best of us.\" I mean, it doesn't. I've never dropped a tray of glasses. But she just wants everyone to like her. She brings in these awful store-bought cupcakes for everyone's birthday, the ones with the frosting that tastes like pure shortening. I always throw mine away when she's not looking. I actually love baking, though. I make this really incredible lemon pound cake. I use real vanilla bean and fresh lemon zest, and I soak it in a lemon syrup while it's still warm. My grandmother taught me the recipe. She used to bake all the time when I was little. Her kitchen always smelled like butter and sugar. She had this huge old stand mixer that weighed like forty pounds... \n\nBut no, a doctor saying it's fine wouldn't change anything. It wouldn't change what I actually see when I look at myself. I know what's there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's anything you could do to change or improve your hairline and face to make them look the way you want?\n\nPatient: I don't know if I can ever really fix it, but I... I definitely try to hide it. I mean, I have to, especially at work. Before a shift, I get stuck in the bathroom just checking the mirror over and over. I take all these pictures on my phone from different angles, trying to see how bad the lighting makes it look, and I keep combing my hair forward, trying to cover the worst spots. I just start replaying in my head exactly how the guests are going to see me when they walk in. I get so worked up doing this that I end up running late, or I'm just too anxious to even go out to the host stand. \n\nIt's actually really stressful being the first person people see when they walk into a restaurant. You have to be so \"on\" all the time. Last week, we had this massive party of twelve come in right at the dinner rush without a reservation. They just expected us to push a bunch of tables together, which completely messes up the floor plan and throws off the servers' sections. I had to explain to this guy—he was wearing this incredibly loud, patterned golf shirt—that we couldn't accommodate them for at least an hour. He got so defensive. People get really weird when they're hungry. My dad used to get like that. If dinner wasn't on the table by 6:00 PM on the dot, he'd start pacing around the kitchen, opening and closing the fridge. He was a mechanic, so he was always coming home starving and covered in grease. He had this old Ford pickup truck that he spent more time fixing than actually driving. We used to take it out to the lake on weekends when I was a kid, just me and him, and we'd fish for hours. I never actually liked fishing, to be honest. Staring at the water just made me dizzy... \n\nBut yeah, I try to fix it, or at least manage it, before I go out front. But it never really feels like it works.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your appearance would be considered normal by someone who has never seen you before and has no reason to be polite or try to make you feel better?\n\nPatient: No, absolutely not. A stranger wouldn't think I look normal. They'd see exactly what I see. I'm here because I can't stop thinking about it. My hairline, and the way my face looks, especially under those harsh restaurant lights... it just makes me look so old and strange. My coworkers tell me they don't see what I'm talking about, but they're just being polite, like we talked about. They have to work with me, so they aren't going to say, \"Wow, you look terrible today.\" \n\nThose lights at work are the absolute worst, though. They're these awful, bright halogen bulbs that hang right over the host stand. The owner, Mr. Peterson, installed them last year because he said the entryway was too \"moody\" and people couldn't read the menus. He's always making these bizarre design choices. He bought these velvet chairs for the lounge area that look like they belong in a 1970s casino, and they are impossible to clean. If someone spills a martini on them, the stain is just there forever. I remember back in college, I had this roommate, Dave, who spilled an entire pot of chili on our living room rug. It was this cheap shag rug we got at a thrift store, and it smelled like cumin for the rest of the semester. Dave was a disaster. He was a theater major, always walking around the apartment doing vocal warm-ups at two in the morning. \"Red leather, yellow leather,\" over and over again while I was trying to study for my accounting exams. I started out as an accounting major, you know. I thought I wanted to be a CPA. I liked the idea of everything balancing out perfectly at the end of the day. Numbers always make sense. But I couldn't pass intermediate accounting to save my life, so I dropped out... \n\nBut anyway, no. A stranger would take one look at my hairline and my face under those lights and think I look completely abnormal. There's no way they wouldn't.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your appearance would change if you stopped thinking about it?\n\nPatient: I... I don't know. I mean, how could I even test that? I can't just stop thinking about it, so I really have no idea if it would physically change anything. My brain just doesn't work that way. \n\nIt's like when someone tells you not to think about a pink elephant, right? You just end up thinking about it more. I read this article once about how the human brain processes negative commands. It was in one of those old magazines at the dentist's office. I had to get a crown replaced last month. The dentist, Dr. Evans, he's been doing my teeth for fifteen years, but his new hygienist is terribly rough. She was scraping at my gums like she was trying to dig up buried treasure. I almost said something, but I hate causing a scene in professional settings. \n\nIt's just bad etiquette. Like the new hosts we hire at the restaurant. They have zero situational awareness. If the iPad reservation system glitches for even a second, they completely panic and just freeze up in front of the guests, or they start looking around frantically for me to fix it. Kids today just don't know how to handle a tiny bit of pressure. They want everything to be perfectly smooth all the time. I tried to explain to this one girl, Chloe, that you just have to smile and make small talk while the system reboots. Just ask them about their day, you know? But she just stared at me like I was speaking a foreign language. She's always on her phone anyway, probably on TikTok or whatever it is they do. \n\nBut, uh... wait, what were you asking? Oh, if my appearance would change if I stopped focusing on it. Yeah, I honestly don't know. I just know I can't turn it off.\n\nDoctor: Do you think a photograph of you would show the same flaws you see when you look in the mirror?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. Honestly, pictures are almost worse than the mirror. That's... I mean, that's really why I'm here. I just can't stop thinking about it. Especially because of those restaurant lights I mentioned earlier, they just make me look so old and completely strange. It is absolutely terrifying. She set up this massive, wobbly vat of boiling peanut oil right on her wooden deck. I kept telling her, \"Brenda, that is a massive fire hazard, the wood is completely dry,\" but she never listens to me. She's been stubborn like that since we were kids. Always doing things the most chaotic way possible. Like when we were teenagers, she tried to dye her own hair with cherry Kool-Aid and it stained the porcelain bathtub bright pink for about six months. My mom was absolutely furious. She tried bleaching it, scrubbing it with baking soda... nothing worked. We just had to take pink showers until it finally faded. \n\nBut, um... wait, what were you asking? Oh, right, photographs. Yeah. If someone took a normal picture of me, without all those crazy filters, it would definitely show exactly how strange I look.\n\nDoctor: Do you think a professional photographer, using a neutral lighting setup, would capture the flaws in your appearance that you're concerned about?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... a professional photographer? My sister Brenda—the one with the turkey disaster—she hired this guy for her daughter's senior portraits last spring. The guy charged, like, eight hundred dollars for an hour at the botanical gardens. And he didn't even print the photos! He just gave her a link to a digital folder. When did that become the standard? Back in the nineties, you went to Olan Mills at the mall, you sat in front of a fake laser background, and you walked out with a sheet of wallet-sized prints to hand out to your friends. It was simple. I remember my senior picture, I wore this awful mustard-yellow sweater my grandmother knitted for me. It was so itchy, I had this red rash on my neck the whole day. Grandma meant well, but she used this really cheap acrylic yarn she bought at a discount bin at JoAnn Fabrics. She was always looking for a bargain. She used to save the rubber bands from the newspaper delivery and keep them in a giant ball in the kitchen drawer. Like my manager at the restaurant, Brenda. She's always telling everyone they're doing a \"fantastic job,\" even when they're clearly messing up. Last week, one of the new bussers dropped an entire tray of water glasses right in the middle of the dining room. Shattered everywhere. It took us twenty minutes to clean it up, and we had to comp two tables' appetizers because they got splashed. And Brenda just patted him on the shoulder and said, \"Don't worry, it happens to the best of us.\" I mean, it doesn't. I've never dropped a tray of glasses. But she just wants everyone to like her. She brings in these awful store-bought cupcakes for everyone's birthday, the ones with the frosting that tastes like pure shortening. I always throw mine away when she's not looking. I actually love baking, though. I make this really incredible lemon pound cake. I use real vanilla bean and fresh lemon zest, and I soak it in a lemon syrup while it's still warm. My grandmother taught me the recipe. She used to bake all the time when I was little. Her kitchen always smelled like butter and sugar. She had this huge old stand mixer that weighed like forty pounds... \n\nBut no, a doctor saying it's fine wouldn't change anything. It wouldn't change what I actually see when I look at myself. I know what's there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's anything you could do to change or improve your hairline and face to make them look the way you want?\n\nPatient: I don't know if I can ever really fix it, but I... I definitely try to hide it. I mean, I have to, especially at work. Before a shift, I get stuck in the bathroom just checking the mirror over and over. I take all these pictures on my phone from different angles, trying to see how bad the lighting makes it look, and I keep combing my hair forward, trying to cover the worst spots. I just start replaying in my head exactly how the guests are going to see me when they walk in. I get so worked up doing this that I end up running late, or I'm just too anxious to even go out to the host stand. \n\nIt's actually really stressful being the first person people see when they walk into a restaurant. You have to be so \"on\" all the time. Last week, we had this massive party of twelve come in right at the dinner rush without a reservation. They just expected us to push a bunch of tables together, which completely messes up the floor plan and throws off the servers' sections. I had to explain to this guy—he was wearing this incredibly loud, patterned golf shirt—that we couldn't accommodate them for at least an hour. He got so defensive. People get really weird when they're hungry. My dad used to get like that. If dinner wasn't on the table by 6:00 PM on the dot, he'd start pacing around the kitchen, opening and closing the fridge. He was a mechanic, so he was always coming home starving and covered in grease. He had this old Ford pickup truck that he spent more time fixing than actually driving. We used to take it out to the lake on weekends when I was a kid, just me and him, and we'd fish for hours. I never actually liked fishing, to be honest. Staring at the water just made me dizzy... \n\nBut yeah, I try to fix it, or at least manage it, before I go out front. But it never really feels like it works.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your appearance would be considered normal by someone who has never seen you before and has no reason to be polite or try to make you feel better?\n\nPatient: No, absolutely not. A stranger wouldn't think I look normal. They'd see exactly what I see. I'm here because I can't stop thinking about it. My hairline, and the way my face looks, especially under those harsh restaurant lights... it just makes me look so old and strange. My coworkers tell me they don't see what I'm talking about, but they're just being polite, like we talked about. They have to work with me, so they aren't going to say, \"Wow, you look terrible today.\" \n\nThose lights at work are the absolute worst, though. They're these awful, bright halogen bulbs that hang right over the host stand. The owner, Mr. Peterson, installed them last year because he said the entryway was too \"moody\" and people couldn't read the menus. He's always making these bizarre design choices. He bought these velvet chairs for the lounge area that look like they belong in a 1970s casino, and they are impossible to clean. If someone spills a martini on them, the stain is just there forever. I remember back in college, I had this roommate, Dave, who spilled an entire pot of chili on our living room rug. It was this cheap shag rug we got at a thrift store, and it smelled like cumin for the rest of the semester. Dave was a disaster. He was a theater major, always walking around the apartment doing vocal warm-ups at two in the morning. \"Red leather, yellow leather,\" over and over again while I was trying to study for my accounting exams. I started out as an accounting major, you know. I thought I wanted to be a CPA. I liked the idea of everything balancing out perfectly at the end of the day. Numbers always make sense. But I couldn't pass intermediate accounting to save my life, so I dropped out... \n\nBut anyway, no. A stranger would take one look at my hairline and my face under those lights and think I look completely abnormal. There's no way they wouldn't.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your appearance would change if you stopped thinking about it?\n\nPatient: I... I don't know. I mean, how could I even test that? I can't just stop thinking about it, so I really have no idea if it would physically change anything. My brain just doesn't work that way. \n\nIt's like when someone tells you not to think about a pink elephant, right? You just end up thinking about it more. I read this article once about how the human brain processes negative commands. It was in one of those old magazines at the dentist's office. I had to get a crown replaced last month. The dentist, Dr. Evans, he's been doing my teeth for fifteen years, but his new hygienist is terribly rough. She was scraping at my gums like she was trying to dig up buried treasure. I almost said something, but I hate causing a scene in professional settings. \n\nIt's just bad etiquette. Like the new hosts we hire at the restaurant. They have zero situational awareness. If the iPad reservation system glitches for even a second, they completely panic and just freeze up in front of the guests, or they start looking around frantically for me to fix it. Kids today just don't know how to handle a tiny bit of pressure. They want everything to be perfectly smooth all the time. I tried to explain to this one girl, Chloe, that you just have to smile and make small talk while the system reboots. Just ask them about their day, you know? But she just stared at me like I was speaking a foreign language. She's always on her phone anyway, probably on TikTok or whatever it is they do. \n\nBut, uh... wait, what were you asking? Oh, if my appearance would change if I stopped focusing on it. Yeah, I honestly don't know. I just know I can't turn it off.\n\nDoctor: Do you think a photograph of you would show the same flaws you see when you look in the mirror?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. Honestly, pictures are almost worse than the mirror. That's... I mean, that's really why I'm here. I just can't stop thinking about it. No matter if it's a photo or a mirror, I just constantly fixate on my hairline, and the way my face looks. Especially because of those restaurant lights I mentioned earlier, they just make me look so old and completely strange. And even though my coworkers tell me they don't see what I'm talking about, I know it's there. The camera captures exactly what I see. \n\nThough, you know, nobody takes real pictures anymore anyway. Everything is filtered. My niece, Sarah, she was showing me this app on her phone at Thanksgiving where it completely smooths out your skin and changes your jawline. Thanksgiving was a total nightmare last year, by the way. My sister, Brenda, she insisted on deep-frying the turkey. Have you ever seen someone try to deep-fry a twenty-pound bird? It is absolutely terrifying. She set up this massive, wobbly vat of boiling peanut oil right on her wooden deck. I kept telling her, \"Brenda, that is a massive fire hazard, the wood is completely dry,\" but she never listens to me. She's been stubborn like that since we were kids. Always doing things the most chaotic way possible. Like when we were teenagers, she tried to dye her own hair with cherry Kool-Aid and it stained the porcelain bathtub bright pink for about six months. My mom was absolutely furious. She tried bleaching it, scrubbing it with baking soda... nothing worked. We just had to take pink showers until it finally faded. \n\nBut, um... wait, what were you asking? Oh, right, photographs. Yeah. If someone took a normal picture of me, without all those crazy filters, it would definitely show exactly how strange I look.\n\nDoctor: Do you think a professional photographer, using a neutral lighting setup, would capture the flaws in your appearance that you're concerned about?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... a professional photographer? Sure, they'd capture it. Neutral lighting, harsh lighting, it doesn't matter. The flaws are there, so the camera's going to pick them up. It's not like a trick of the light, it's just... my face. My hairline. \n\nThough, you know, professional photographers are so expensive these days. My sister Brenda—the one with the turkey disaster—she hired this guy for her daughter's senior portraits last spring. The guy charged, like, eight hundred dollars for an hour at the botanical gardens. And he didn't even print the photos! He just gave her a link to a digital folder. When did that become the standard? Back in the nineties, you went to Olan Mills at the mall, you sat in front of a fake laser background, and you walked out with a sheet of wallet-sized prints to hand out to your friends. It was simple. I remember my senior picture, I wore this awful mustard-yellow sweater my grandmother knitted for me. It was so itchy, I had this red rash on my neck the whole day. Grandma meant well, but she used this really cheap acrylic yarn she bought at a discount bin at JoAnn Fabrics. She was always looking for a bargain. She used to save the rubber bands from the newspaper delivery and keep them in a giant ball in the kitchen drawer. It got to be the size of a grapefruit before she finally threw it out. \n\nBut, uh... anyway. Yeah. A professional photographer would definitely see it. They might even see it worse, since their cameras are so high-resolution now. They pick up every single terrible detail.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I don't know, honestly. I haven't really asked one. I mean, they're doctors, so maybe they'd just see it as a normal aging thing, or maybe they'd see exactly how awful and abnormal it really is. I've thought about going to a dermatologist, actually. But getting a specialist appointment around here is basically impossible. 

I tried to get my husband into an ear, nose, and throat doctor last month because he snores so loudly it shakes the bed frame. I'm not exaggerating. It sounds like a freight train is running through our bedroom every night at 2 AM. Anyway, I called the clinic, and the receptionist put me on hold for literally forty-five minutes. And they had this terrible hold music playing, just a ten-second loop of a saxophone over and over again. It drove me absolutely insane. It reminded me of this jazz club we went to for our anniversary about five years ago. We thought it would be romantic, you know? Dim lighting, cocktails. But the band was so loud we couldn't even hear each other speak, and the drinks were ridiculously overpriced. Eighteen dollars for a tiny martini! And it wasn't even good gin. You can always tell when a place uses cheap well liquor. Working in a restaurant, you pick up on those things. Like our bartender, Luis, he's great, but he has this habit of over-pouring the simple syrup when we get busy. I have to keep reminding him that a mojito shouldn't taste like a melted popsicle. 

But, um... wait, a dermatologist? Yeah, I don't know what they would say. I just know what I see in the mirror every day, and it's just... it's not right.
